## B.10 Enhancing Energy efficiency

The Government of Kenya (GoK) is committed to the implementation of the Paris Agreement through its National Determined Contributions (NDCs). The GoK has developed a Green Growth and Climate Resilience Strategy (GGCRS) that outlines the country's commitment to sustainable development, climate change mitigation, and adaptation. The GGCRS aims to enhance energy efficiency in various sectors, including agriculture, industry, and transport.

The Enhancing Energy Efficiency for the Implementation of Competitive Funds (Project 10) will be implemented by the Ministry of Energy (MoE). This project aims to improve energy efficiency in key sectors such as agriculture, industry, and transport. The project will also support the development of policies and regulations that promote energy efficiency.

The project will be implemented through a combination of policy reforms, capacity building, and investment in energy-efficient technologies. The MoE will work with other government agencies, including the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock Industry and Fisheries (MoAILF), to implement the project. The project will also involve the private sector and civil society organizations to ensure that it is implemented effectively.

The project will be implemented over a period of five years, with an initial phase of one year for policy development and capacity building. The second phase will focus on investment in energy-efficient technologies, while the third phase will focus on monitoring and evaluation.

## K.31 Ex-ante screening

[Text not visible]

### JOPs 2.6 and 6.5 in Kenya, Nigeria, Tanzania, and Uganda: indirect employment changes in production and consumption activities due to climate change mitigation and adaptation measures

The project aims to assess the impact of climate change on agriculture, livestock, and fisheries sectors in Kenya, Nigeria, Tanzania, and Uganda. The project will also evaluate the potential for climate change mitigation and adaptation measures to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and enhance carbon sequestration.

The project will be implemented over a period of three years, with an initial phase of one year for data collection and analysis. The second phase will focus on the development of policy recommendations and strategies for climate change mitigation and adaptation. The third phase will focus on the implementation of these measures and the monitoring and evaluation of their impact.

The project will be implemented by a consortium of research institutions, government agencies, and non-governmental organizations in Kenya, Nigeria, Tanzania, and Uganda. The project will also involve the private sector to ensure that it is implemented effectively.

## B

### Financing and Upgrade: the capital power of the dry zone makes this a unique emission and emission reduction opportunity. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is well suited to finance climate change mitigation in national parks, wildlife sanctuaries, and protected areas. This proposal is consistent with the objectives of the GCF and the priorities identified by the Government of Sri Lanka (GoSL). This aligns with the GoSL's Intended Nationally Determined Contributions (INDC) and the National Adaptation Plan Framework (NAPF).

### Program 1: Strategies and Implementation Approaches

The program will focus on developing strategies to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, promote sustainable management of forests, enhance forest carbon stocks, increase ecosystem resilience, and support the conservation and sustainable use of biodiversity. The program will also aim to improve the livelihoods of local communities by providing them with access to clean energy and promoting sustainable practices.

### Component 1: The Green Climate Fund in Sri Lanka

The GCF will provide funding for a project that aims to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, promote sustainable management of forests, enhance forest carbon stocks, increase ecosystem resilience, and support the conservation and sustainable use of biodiversity. The program will also aim to improve the livelihoods of local communities by providing them with access to clean energy and promoting sustainable practices.

### Component 2: Sustainable Dry Zones and Landscapes

This component aims to promote sustainable dry zone management and development. It will focus on improving the resilience of ecosystems, promoting sustainable land use, and enhancing the livelihoods of local communities. The program will also aim to support the conservation and sustainable use of biodiversity.

### Component 3: Capacity Building and Knowledge Management

This component aims to build the capacity of national and regional institutions, utilities, and other stakeholders to implement climate-smart practices. It will provide training, technical assistance, and knowledge management services to support the implementation of the program.

### Component 4: Implementation Arrangements

The Accredited Entity (AE) for the Programme, NDC, will be responsible for implementing the programme. The AE will work with the GoSL to ensure that the programme is implemented effectively and efficiently. The AE will also provide technical assistance and capacity building support to the GoSL.

### Component 5: Monitoring and Evaluation

The programme will include a comprehensive monitoring and evaluation system to track progress towards its objectives. This will include regular reporting, data collection, and analysis to ensure that the programme is achieving its intended outcomes.

### 7. Programme costs and financing

### 9. Replenishment and ingredients

The Programme will be replenished through a combination of the following sources:

- **Government of Kenya (GoK)**: USD 84.70 million
- **Private Sector**: USD 100.00 million
- **International Financial Institutions (IFIs)**: USD 250.00 million

### 10. Economic and financial analysis

The financial analysis shows that the programme is expected to generate significant economic benefits. The total net present value of the programme is estimated at USD 987.52 million, which represents a benefit-cost ratio (BCR) of 1.63. The internal rate of return (IRR) is 14.0%. The programme is expected to create approximately 1,000 jobs and generate USD 100.00 million in additional tax revenue for the Government of Kenya.

### 11. Programme risk

The Programme will be subject to a number of risks that could affect its implementation and outcomes. These include:

- **Political Risk**: Changes in government policies or priorities could affect the programme's implementation.
- **Financial Risk**: Fluctuations in exchange rates or interest rates could affect the programme's financing.
- **Operational Risk**: Delays in procurement, construction, or other activities could affect the programme's timeline.

The Programme will be subject to regular monitoring and evaluation to identify and mitigate these risks. The Government of Kenya will also work with international partners to ensure that the programme is implemented effectively and efficiently.

# B. PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Country context

### B.1.1 Kenya: Z-Floods (Risk)

12. Smallholder farmers dominate agriculture in East Africa. They rely heavily on livestock for their livelihoods', most particularly dairy, which is based on wet season pastures and dry season agro-pastoralism, as allowed by the climate. The Kenyan government has invested significantly in climate-smart agriculture (CSA) to enhance resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change. The Government has also developed a National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP), which aims to reduce vulnerability to climate risks through targeted adaptation actions and long-term investments. The NCCAP is aligned with the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) and the Paris Agreement, and has been integrated into the country's development plans.

13. The dairy sector in Kenya is diverse, ranging from smallholder farmers to large-scale commercial operations. The sector contributes significantly to GDP and is characterized by high GDP elasticity, especially in rural areas. The sector also supports eight CC objectives: (i) enhancing climate resilience; (ii) increasing productivity and efficiency; (iii) reducing greenhouse gas emissions; (iv) promoting sustainable use of natural resources; (v) improving water management; (vi) enhancing food security; (vii) promoting sustainable livelihoods; and (viii) enhancing climate information and early warning systems. The sector is also a major contributor to the economy, accounting for 40% of agricultural GDP.

### CLIMATE HAZARDS

- **Drought**: Droughts are becoming more frequent and severe, exposing dairy farmers' livelihoods to a high risk of failure. Climate change poses risks both because the impacts on weather variability and water availability increase drought frequency and severity.
- **Flooding**: Flooding is also increasing in frequency and intensity due to climate change. This has led to increased soil erosion and loss of pastureland, which negatively affects livestock productivity.

### CLIMATE CHANGE IMPACTS

- **Temperature changes**: Increased temperatures have led to reduced milk yields and increased mortality rates among dairy cattle.
- **Precipitation changes**: Changes in precipitation patterns have affected feed availability and water resources for livestock.
- **Extreme weather events**: Extreme weather events, such as droughts and floods, have negatively impacted the dairy sector's productivity and profitability.

### CLIMATE CHANGE MITIGATION AND ADAPTATION MEASURES

- **Government initiatives**: The Kenyan government has implemented various measures to mitigate and adapt to climate change, including the development of the NCCAP.
- **Private sector engagement**: Private sector companies have also invested in climate-smart agriculture practices to enhance resilience and productivity.

### FUNDING REQUIREMENTS

- **Total funding required**: USD 10 million over five years.
- **Funding sources**: The funding will be sourced from both public and private sector investments, as well as international development assistance.

## B

### Natural responses to modulate heat stress: The analysis of function and energy expenses to maintain and protect physiological processes.

#### Observed climate change

15. **Precipitation** decreased four out of the five quarters during the 1980s (4.7mm/y) and the 1990s (2.3mm/y), but increased in the first quarter of the 2000s (6.7mm/y). The highest precipitation was observed from January to April, with a maximum of 5.9 mm/day over 189-2010. On the other hand, a precipitation increase has also been observed in the last decade.

![Figure 1](#)

**January-March (JFM)**

**April-June (AMJ)**

**July-September (JAS)**

**October-November (OND)**

**Temperature**

The **temperature** data were collected from the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) and the Kenya Meteorological Department. In Uganda, Eastern Africa, and Tanzania, the temperature has increased by 0.5°C over the last century. The highest temperatures have been recorded in the northern part of Lake Victoria, with a maximum of 32.8°C. The mean annual temperature has increased from 19.6°C to 24.7°C over the past decade.

17. **Mean-water humidity**: The dew point is strongly correlated with the lowest average monthly relative humidity (RH) values in Uganda. The highest RH values were recorded during the dry season, with a maximum of 85% and a minimum of 60%. The mean annual RH has increased from 49.3% to 72.1%, indicating an increase in water vapor.

18. **Radiant variability**: The reported climatic seasons are influenced by a coupled transboundary ocean-atmosphere system that affects the East African region. The highest temperatures were recorded between May (JAM) and July (JAS), while the lowest were from October to December (OND). The highest RH values were also observed in OND, with a maximum of 85%. The mean annual temperature has increased by 0.6°C over the past decade.

*References:*

- Charles, R.A., et al. (2013). Impacts and future variability of temperature and precipitation over East Africa. Nature Climate Change.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL v1.0 PAGE 8

## Extreme weather events

### Droughts

The average annual number of dry days (R1w) in East Africa exceeds 250 days. The frequency and intensity of droughts have increased over the past decade, with significant impacts on agriculture, water resources, and food security. The 2011 drought in Kenya, for example, resulted in a loss of 30% of livestock and reduced crop yields by up to 70%. Droughts also exacerbate existing social tensions and conflicts, particularly in areas where access to water is limited.

### Floods

Floods are becoming more frequent and severe. The average annual number of days with floods (R1w) has increased from 35 days in the 1980s to over 60 days in recent years. This trend is expected to continue, with the frequency and intensity of floods increasing due to changes in precipitation patterns.

## Extreme temperatures

Most of the climate models indicate an increase in the number of very warm days (TXx) over the next few decades. The frequency and intensity of heatwaves are expected to increase significantly, with a projected increase of up to 50% by the end of the century.

### Projected climate change

- Global temperature is projected to rise by 1.5°C between 2030 and 2052 if greenhouse gas emissions continue at their current rate.
- Sea levels are expected to rise by 0.26 to 0.77 meters by the end of the century.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL v1.0: PAGE 8

## Annual precipitation

Some parts of East Africa may experience an increase in rainfall in the distant future (2081-2100), but this is not expected to be enough to offset the increased temperatures and drought stress that will reduce crop yields. Dry seasons in Kenya, for example, are projected to become longer and more severe under RCP 6.0 (+10 days) and RCP 8.5 (+19 days). By 2081-2100, annual precipitation is expected to decrease by up to 30% in the drought prone areas of Kenya (RCP 8.5: -17%; RCP 6.0: -14%). In Uganda, annual precipitation under a business-as-usual scenario is projected to increase by up to 20%.

![Figure 4](image_url)

## Mean maximum temperatures

Under RCP 6.0, mean Temperatures are expected to peak in the mid-2080s and then decline slightly towards the end of the century. Under RCP 8.5, mean Temperatures are projected to increase by up to 3.9°C (1971-2000 to 2081-2100) in East Africa. This may lead to a significant increase in the frequency and severity of heatwaves. The results indicate that the climate change impacts on agriculture will be severe, particularly for smallholder farmers who are already vulnerable.

![Figure 5](image_url)

## B

### Main climate temperature

The West African Tree increase over East Africa is reported to have the same trend as the global mean temperature. The observed mean annual temperature in West Africa has increased from 26.5°C in 1980 to 27.3°C in 2014, an increase of 0.8°C over a period of 34 years. This is consistent with the global trend.

### Mean relative humidity (mean RH)

Mean relative humidity (mean RH) will change over the course of the century for RCP 2.6 and 8.5. The mean annual relative humidity in West Africa has decreased by about 10% from 79% to 69%. This is expected to decrease on a yearly average (2-4%) in the central parts of Tanzania, with a higher trend.

### Potential evapotranspiration

Potential evapotranspiration is a measure that varies RCP 8.5 shows an increase over RCP 2.6 and RCP 6.0. For negative RCP 3.0 shows dry periods, negative values are associated with the El Niño events. The potential evapotranspiration has increased by about 10% in West Africa.

### Dust species

Dust species have a positive agreement in the time of emergence on the number of dry days during the rainy season and wet days of dry seasons. This is consistent with the observed increase in dust storms over East Africa. The dust species have been observed to increase by about 10% in West Africa.

### Exposure

Exposure is the presence of people, habitats, species, and ecosystems that would be affected by hazards.

#### Direct Health Impacts

Direct health impacts include deaths and injuries from extreme heat. Heat-related illnesses can cause death in vulnerable populations and increase the risk of cardiovascular and respiratory diseases. Extreme heat can also exacerbate existing conditions such as diabetes and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD). Heat stress can lead to increased morbidity, decreased life expectancy, and an increased presence of drought-related health issues.

#### Indirect Health Impacts

Indirect health impacts include changes in the distribution and prevalence of vector-borne diseases. For example, the spread of malaria and dengue fever is closely linked to temperature and precipitation patterns. Warmer temperatures can extend the breeding season of vectors such as mosquitoes, leading to an increased risk of disease transmission.

#### References

1. Avery Anne E. *Extreme Heat*. The Effects of Droughts on Food Security in Farmers. The International Journal of Climate Change: Impacts and Responses, 2013.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## B

### a) Poverty and Inequality
Kazakhstan, Tanzania, Rwanda and Uganda experience are noted by the World Bank as countries with high levels of poverty and inequality. In Kazakhstan, 18.4% of the population lives on less than $2.50 a day in urban areas (43.7% in rural areas), 6.9% in Tanzania, and 27.7% in Uganda live below the same threshold. Inequality is also high in these countries.

### b) Dependence of livelihoods on fragile ecosystems
Livelihoods are dependent on degraded and increasingly fragile ecosystems for a significant proportion of the population. This is particularly true in rural areas where people depend on natural resources for their livelihoods. In many cases, this dependence is exacerbated by climate change impacts such as drought, floods, and other extreme weather events.

### c) Limited access to quality inputs
Farmers have limited access to quality seeds, fertilizers, and other agricultural inputs. Limited infrastructure prevents land consolidation and the adoption of modern farming techniques. In addition, limited access to credit prevents farmers from investing in new technologies and practices that could improve productivity and increase profitability, especially for women who often provide labor.

### d) Limited market integration
Farmers have limited access to markets due to poor infrastructure and lack of information about prices and demand. This limits their ability to sell their products at a fair price and to access new markets. In addition, limited access to credit prevents farmers from investing in new technologies and practices that could improve productivity and increase profitability.

### e) Frequent disease outbreaks
East Africa is prone to frequent disease outbreaks such as Rift Valley Fever (RVF), which is common in Kenya, Tanzania, Rwanda, and Uganda. RVF is a viral disease of livestock and humans caused by the RVF virus. The disease is transmitted by mosquitoes and other blood-sucking insects. Poor sanitation and lack of clean water also contribute to the spread of diseases.

### f) Climate Change
Climate change is a major threat to food security in East Africa. Rising temperatures, changes in precipitation patterns, and increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events are expected to have significant impacts on agriculture, water resources, and ecosystems. These changes will affect the livelihoods of millions of people who depend on agriculture for their livelihoods.

---

### FAO/IFAD
The project aims to improve food security by increasing agricultural productivity and incomes in rural areas. The project is implemented through a combination of investments in infrastructure, technology, and capacity building. The project is expected to benefit 10,000 households in the target countries.

### World Bank
The World Bank's climate change program focuses on improving the resilience of vulnerable communities to the impacts of climate change. The program includes investments in early warning systems, disaster risk management, and adaptation measures. The program also supports the development of policies and institutions that can effectively respond to the challenges posed by climate change.

### African Development Fund
The African Development Fund is a concessional lending window of the African Development Bank Group (AfDB) that provides low-interest loans and grants for projects that support economic and social development in Africa. The fund focuses on supporting projects that promote inclusive growth, improve living standards, and enhance the competitiveness of African countries.

## B

![Map](image-url)

- **Daily processing facilities, energy supply and access to markets**: Daily farming in East Africa boosts local economies by providing jobs and income. However, it is also impacted by weather conditions that affect the ability to use and process milk and livestock products. Access to clean water and electricity is limited due to poor infrastructure. The lack of efficient daily processing facilities affects farmers' livelihoods.

- **Limited access to credit and financial services**: Critical aspects of dairy farming, such as infrastructure development, are hindered by limited access to credit and financial services. This includes the inability to purchase necessary equipment for farming, which is often due to lack of collateral, which in turn limits their ability to secure loans from banks.

- **Land tenure in Kenya, Tanzania and Uganda**: A main risk for increased grazing is tenancy. In Kenya, land tenure is highly insecure, with most farmers leasing land on a short-term basis. This insecurity affects the ability to invest in long-term resources and the adoption of climate-smart practices.

### CLIMATE ADAPTIVE CAPACITY

- **About 70-90 percent East African agriculture is rain-fed**: Small-scale farmers relying on seasonal rainfall patterns are highly vulnerable to droughts, floods, and other extreme weather events. Climate change exacerbates these risks by increasing the frequency of such events.

- **Rising temperatures are damaging populations**: Increasing and daily, the economic potential of East Africa, water supplies, pastures, and grazing livestock, and reducing productivity. Increased food and nutrition security, droughts, and floods have significant impacts on local communities' livelihoods. The impact of climate change is expected to increase in the coming years.

  - This includes developing policy frameworks and regulations.

## IMPACT OF CLIMATE CHANGE

Climate change is one of the most substantive factors to climate change. The impact of which are felt at a local level of the dairy value chain. The impacts at the farm level have been identified as a decline in milk production, reduced feed quality, and increased incidence of diseases. These changes can be attributed to changes in temperature and rainfall patterns. Climate change can also affect the productivity of the dairy sector by reducing the number of animals that can be supported per unit area.

According to FAO, climate change is expected to reduce global agricultural output by 2% each year. This will have a significant impact on the business and lead to a loss in productivity.

### According to FAO

Climate change is expected to reduce global agricultural output by 2% each year. This will have a significant impact on the business and lead to a loss in productivity.

### According to IPCC

Indicates climate change is the greatest threat nearly than a third of the evidence in Global Food Security.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## B

### Climate change will affect people

Unemployment and income loss have a direct impact on well-being and are associated with anxiety, depression, substance abuse, domestic violence, family instability, and suicide. Climate change is expected to increase the frequency of extreme weather events, such as heat waves, droughts, wildfires, floods, and storms. These events can lead to significant health impacts, including injuries, respiratory illnesses, and deaths. For example, in 2018, a heat wave in Quebec, Canada, resulted in over 90 deaths.

### Remittance flows and foreign supplies are most affected by climate change due to changes in cereal, livestock, and fisheries production

Climate change affects the agricultural sector by altering the timing of seasonal events. For example, changes in temperature and precipitation patterns can lead to shifts in crop yields and harvest times. These changes can have significant impacts on food security and nutrition. For instance, a study found that climate change could reduce global wheat yields by 6% to 20%, rice by 10% to 20%, and maize by 7% to 25%. These reductions would have significant implications for food prices and availability.

### Climate change will affect pathogens, vectors, hosts, or reservoirs

Climate change will also impact human behavior and social interactions. For example, changes in temperature and precipitation patterns can alter the distribution of disease vectors such as mosquitoes, which can lead to an increase in the incidence of diseases such as malaria and dengue fever. Additionally, climate change can affect the availability and quality of water resources, which can have significant impacts on public health.

### References

- IPCC (2014). Climate Change 2014: Impacts, Adaptation, and Vulnerability. Part A: Global and Sectoral Risks. Contribution of Working Group II to the Fifth Assessment Report of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change.
- World Health Organization (WHO) (2018). Climate Change and Health. Fact Sheet.
- IPCC (2019). Special Report on the Ocean and Cryosphere in a Changing Climate.

#### 50

The above impacts of climate change will result in production at farm level and have a direct impact on the greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from livestock systems. The GHG emissions from livestock are influenced by factors such as feed quality, housing, and waste management. A higher temperature will lead to higher intake with an international travel and ensure poultry rearing. Also, a higher temperature will increase the risk of diseases in animals.

#### 51

##### GREENHOUSE-GAS EMISSION PROFILE OF THE DAIRY SECTOR

Daily production systems: 97% (many of which is produced by intensive systems or zero-grazing) and 3% (extensive systems). The dairy sector is divided into three sub-sectors:

- Dairy production in intensive grazing systems
- Dairy production in semi-intensive systems
- Dairy production in extensive systems

![Figure 18: Livestock production systems - See Above](#)

#### 52

##### Mitigated GHG emissions (Beef cattle, poultry, and housing; Emissions CH4, N2O, and CO2)

GHG emissions can be reduced by improving feed quality, reducing methane emissions from enteric fermentation and through manure management. The use of vaccines to reduce the incidence of diseases in animals will also help to reduce GHG emissions.

- The introduction of Jatropha curcas (JD) as a supplement for ruminants has been shown to reduce CH4 emissions by 10-25%.
- The use of JD as a supplement for poultry has been shown to reduce N2O emissions by up to 30%.

#### 53

##### Improved Forage Production (Silage)

The introduction of JD as a supplement for ruminants has been shown to reduce CH4 emissions by 10-25%. The use of JD as a supplement for poultry has been shown to reduce N2O emissions by up to 30%.

42. In Argentina, GHG emissions have increased from 136 MCO2e in 1985 to 148 MCO2e in 1997. Agriculture accounts for all emissions. The GLAM has fixed the fuel and agricultural segments of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) accounting system, which is based on a tiered approach. Argentina has submitted its first national communication to the UNFCCC and has been included in the annual inventory of GHG emissions. The country has also developed a BAU scenario for 2010 and a GHG reduction strategy that includes measures such as improved BAU projections, energy efficiency, renewable energy, reforestation and afforestation, and CH4 management and reporting. The country has also submitted its first national communication to the UNFCCC.

### 47
Exclude biogas methane (CH4). The volatile organic compounds (VOCs) emitted at the farm level of milk, cheese, butter, meat, and other dairy products are measured. This study compares and evaluates dairy production systems with different levels of intensification. The results show that the average emissions from intensive dairy farms in Kenya is 2.8 kg CO2e/kg CMP (milk), while for extensive systems it is 1.5 kg CO2e/kg CMP. This shows the range of emissions depending on the production system and management practices. Farm-level GHG emissions are higher in intensive systems compared to extensive systems.

### 48
In India, the average CH4 is an average 3 kg CO2e/kg milk produced, ranging from 1.4 kg CO2e/kg milk for extensive systems to 5.6 kg CO2e/kg milk for semi-intensive systems and 7.9 kg CO2e/kg milk for intensive systems.

### 49
In Paraguay's dryland, cattle account for 80 percent of enteric methane emissions. Total biogas from 29 dairy farms is estimated at 13.5 m3/day, with an average of 6.7 m3/day per farm. This highlights the importance of regional and national policies to reduce GHG emissions from livestock farming.

### 50
In Tanzania, methane emissions are also measured for 48 enteric CH4 emissions. Most of the animals of dairy cattle are raised in semi-intensive systems. The average CH4 emission is estimated at 3.2 kg CO2e/kg CMP. This shows that the average CH4 emissions from dairy cattle are higher than the reported global average.

## B

and are designed to benefit States (AMR: the use of other values is g. e. from MP Cultures, splits). These proposals will be implemented in a manner that ensures the protection of public welfare and human rights, including the right to development, as well as the promotion and respect for human dignity and of indigenous peoples' rights.

### 54.
On-term solutions for nutrient management and nutrient recycling: These interventions will address the issue of nutrient overload in soils. They include promoting organic matter enrichment through practices that reduce nutrient losses and GHG emissions from agricultural fields. This can be achieved by improving soil health, reducing tillage, and increasing crop residues made in biogas facilities to produce renewable energy for cooking and electricity, as well as nutrient-rich fertilizers.

### 55.
Improvements at the landscape level will address the negative impact of CO2 on ecosystem degradation. These interventions include promoting agroforestry systems that improve soil carbon sequestration, reducing deforestation, and promoting sustainable land management practices. This includes promoting agroforestry systems to foster and enhance the natural carbon sequestration capacity in the bio-systems, and restoring degraded ecosystems through reforestation and afforestation programs.

### 56.
Improvement of the dairy value chain will include the operating agreement MPUs, co-investment points (MPUs), and farming practices. This will include the easy to use and robust same agreements and promote the use of improved breeds and feed management systems. This will also include GHG emissions through the use of probiotics (P) systems as a means to improve the efficiency of dairy production.

### 57.
Improvement by promoting the recycling of waste: The focus will be on the collection, treatment, and disposal of waste. This includes promoting the development of recycling programs that promote the reuse of materials. These interventions will include the promotion of recycling programs that promote the use of recycled materials in the production of new products.

## TARGET AREAS

Clean Air: A complete mapping (Figure 10) has been developed to identify the sources of air pollution that have a significant impact on human health. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V.3.1: PAGE 8

## COUNTRY OWNERSHIP

The Government of Kenya (GoK) acknowledges that MAAP is the core of Kenya's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) and aims to achieve climate change mitigation and adaptation objectives outlined in the NDC. The proposed Programme will support the GoK's efforts to achieve its NDC targets by 2030 through the following activities:

- Enhancing the capacity of MAAP to implement climate change mitigation and adaptation measures.
- Supporting the development and implementation of policies, strategies, and action plans for climate change mitigation and adaptation.
- Strengthening institutional capacities at national and county levels to effectively manage climate change risks.

### Kenya Forestry Act 2005

- The Kenya Forestry Act 2005 provides the legal framework for forest management in Kenya.
- It aims to promote sustainable forest management, conservation, and utilization of forest resources.

### Wildlife Conservation and Management Act 2004

- The Wildlife Conservation and Management Act 2004 provides the legal framework for wildlife conservation in Kenya.
- It aims to protect and manage wildlife species, their habitats, and ecosystems.

The Government of Kenya (GoK) acknowledges that MAAP is the core of Kenya's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) and aims to achieve climate change mitigation and adaptation objectives outlined in the NDC. The proposed Programme will support the GoK's efforts to achieve its NDC targets by 2030 through the following activities:

- Enhancing the capacity of MAAP to implement climate change mitigation and adaptation measures.
- Supporting the development and implementation of policies, strategies, and action plans for climate change mitigation and adaptation.
- Strengthening institutional capacities at national and county levels to effectively manage climate change risks.

In addition, the Government of Kenya (GoK) will work closely with various stakeholders, including international organizations, non-governmental organizations, and private sector entities, to ensure effective implementation and monitoring of the Programme. ## Kenya June 2009

### National Development Appraisal (NDA) (2008)

- **Objective:** To assess the impact of climate change on key sectors and to identify adaptation and mitigation measures.
- **Key findings:**
  - Agriculture: Climate change impacts on crop yields, livestock productivity, and water availability.
  - Water resources: Changes in precipitation patterns affecting water supply.
  - Health: Increased incidence of malaria and other climate-sensitive diseases.

### National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) (2009)

- **Objective:** To identify priority actions to address the immediate impacts of climate change.
- **Key findings:**
  - Agriculture: Need for drought-resistant crops and improved irrigation systems.
  - Water resources: Development of water harvesting techniques.
  - Health: Strengthening health systems to cope with climate-related diseases.

### National Climate Change Response Strategy (NCCRS) (2010)

- **Objective:** To provide a framework for long-term adaptation and mitigation efforts.
- **Key findings:**
  - Agriculture: Promotion of sustainable farming practices.
  - Water resources: Integrated water resource management.
  - Health: Enhancement of health infrastructure to handle climate-related impacts.

### 69
The TFC presented below outlines the intervention logic of the Programme (Figure 14). This section describes how the TFC is implemented at the country level. Interventions are grouped into three categories: (i) those that enhance climate resilience and reduce vulnerability; (ii) those that increase sustainable production and consumption; and (iii) those that enhance capacity to adapt, mitigate and develop resilience. The TFC also includes a section on gender equality and social inclusion. This is to ensure the pathways to the outcomes and ultimate relevant delivery objectives are preserved as much as possible.

### Page for Climate Theory of Change (TfC) diagram

| Category | Intervention Logic |
|----------|---------------------|
| **Enhance climate resilience** | - Reduce risk<br>- Enhance resilience<br>- Increase sustainable production |
| **Increase sustainable production and consumption** | - Promote renewable energies<br>- Sustainable agriculture<br>- Sustainable consumption |
| **Enhance capacity to adapt, mitigate and develop resilience** | - Capacity building<br>- Gender equality<br>- Social inclusion |

---

As highlighted in the TFC, the multi-faceted systems (including planning, knowledge, social, and technical) are fundamental for the successful implementation of the Programme goal. Integrated multiple sectoral approaches are required to achieve the objectives. In practical terms, this means that the changing contexts of deforestation, land tenure, and environmental services need to be considered in the design and implementation of interventions. This includes looking at cross-cutting issues such as gender equality, social inclusion, and human rights. The Programme will also consider the potential for synergies with other initiatives (including those from the private sector) and will draw on lessons learned from these efforts.

Details on the indicators are found in the Feasibility Study.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## B

### Pathways to Low Emission and Resilient Dairy Farming in East Africa

#### 71.
The project seeks feasible significant changes needed to bring changes in East Africa. Dairy farming contributes significantly to the livelihoods of smallholder farmers, but it is also a major source of greenhouse gas emissions. The project aims to reduce these emissions by promoting climate-smart dairy practices that enhance productivity and resilience while reducing environmental impacts.

#### 72.
The Programme aims to address the following challenges: (a) strengthening human resilience; (b) reducing emissions from the dairy sector; and (c) enhancing the capacity of farmers, communities, and other stakeholders. The project will focus on improving feed quality and quantity, adopting efficient farming practices, and promoting the use of low-emission technologies.

#### 73.
The programme also envisions a more efficient, climate-resilient dairy value chain that enhances food security and nutrition. It aims to increase productivity, reduce emissions, and improve the overall efficiency of the dairy sector. The programme will support the adoption of climate-smart practices and technologies that enhance productivity while reducing greenhouse gas emissions.

#### 74.
Outcome 1: Systems and institutional capacities in the dairy sector are strengthened to enable sustainable development and improved livelihoods for smallholder farmers.

#### 75.
Outcome 2 aims to foster an enabling environment for the transformation of the East Africa's dairy sector into a climate-resilient, low-emission, and inclusive value chain.

#### 76.
The Programme will establish a climate-responsive policy and regulatory environment for the dairy sector. It will support the development and implementation of policies that promote sustainable dairy farming practices and enhance the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change.

#### 77.
Outcome 3: Smallholder dairy farmers have improved productivity, reduced emissions, and enhanced livelihoods through adoption of climate-smart technologies, practices, and sustainable practices for increased production and access to markets.

#### 78.
Outcome 4 aims to support the development and implementation of innovative technologies and practices that enhance the resilience of smallholder dairy farmers to climate change. It will also promote the adoption of sustainable farming practices and technologies that reduce greenhouse gas emissions.

#### 79.
Outcome 5: Supporting organizations to develop and implement strategies for their members to adopt climate-smart practices, and to provide technical assistance and capacity building to support these efforts.

## All three components are integrated. Component 1 aims in training national and regional policy and decision makers to understand and apply climate change adaptation and mitigation options, and enhancing regional knowledge exchange. At the same time, Component 3 aims to improve vulnerability and emergency counter (DRR) capacity of local communities through community-based disaster risk management (CBDRM). This component also aims to enhance the resilience of vulnerable groups by promoting gender equality and women empowerment. The synergies among these components are expected to be enhanced through an integrated approach that enables the detailed target benefits a sustainable and resilient future.

## PROGRAMME BENEFICIARIES

The programme will directly benefit 3.5 million people, including 1.8 million women and 200,000 children. The programme will also indirectly benefit an additional 4.5 million people through regional and national policy changes, improved access to climate information services, and enhanced capacity of local governments. Component 1 will directly benefit 300 policy makers at the national and regional levels, as well as 2,000 participants in training workshops. Component 2 will directly benefit 150,000 people through the development and implementation of adaptation plans. Component 3 will directly benefit 3 million people through community-based disaster risk management activities.

## Climate Change Mitigation and Regulatory Framework Establishment

### Objective
Climate change mitigation and regulatory framework establishment, including adaptation measures for climate change adaptation and resilience, is the focus of this proposal. The Programme will support the governments (Beneficiaries) to develop and implement policies and regulations that promote renewable energy, energy efficiency, and climate resilience.

### Activities
- Develop national climate change policies and strategies.
- Establish a regulatory framework for renewable energy and energy efficiency.
- Provide technical assistance to government agencies responsible for implementing climate change policies.
- Conduct public awareness campaigns on the benefits of renewable energy and energy efficiency.
- Support the development of regional cooperation initiatives in East Africa.

### Implementation
The Programme will be implemented over a period of five years, with an initial focus on developing policies and regulations. This will be followed by the implementation of pilot projects and the establishment of monitoring and evaluation systems. The Programme will also support the development of regional cooperation initiatives in East Africa.

### Partners
- United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)
- Global Environment Facility (GEF)
- African Development Bank (AfDB)
- World Bank Group (WBG)

### References
- IPCC AR5: 2014 Synthesis Report: Managing the Risks of Extreme Events and Disasters to Advance Climate Change Adaptation.
- UNFCCC: 2015 Paris Agreement: Adopted at COP 21 in Paris, France.

# Sub-activity 1.3.2.1: Preparation of funding modalities for expanding climate adaptation measures

Sub-activity 1.3.2.1 includes the preparation of funding modalities for expanding climate adaptation measures. This sub-activity will be implemented under the Climate Change Adaptation Project (CCAP) and the National Adaptation Plan (NAP). The objective is to ensure that climate change adaptation measures are funded through various sources, including the Green Climate Fund (GCF), bilateral and multilateral funding, and other available resources. The project aims to enhance the capacity of national and local governments to access these funds and implement adaptation projects effectively.

## Sub-activity 1.3.2.2: Preparation of funding modalities for expanding climate change mitigation efforts

This sub-activity will focus on the preparation of funding modalities for expanding climate change mitigation efforts. The objective is to ensure that mitigation measures are funded through various sources, including the Green Climate Fund (GCF), bilateral and multilateral funding, and other available resources. The project aims to enhance the capacity of national and local governments to access these funds and implement mitigation projects effectively.

## Sub-activity 1.3.2.3: Promote innovative financing solutions

This sub-activity will promote innovative financing solutions through various initiatives such as the Green Climate Fund (GCF), which was piloted in 2015. Results-based financing is another initiative that has been implemented to promote climate change mitigation and adaptation efforts. The project aims to enhance the capacity of national and local governments to access these funds and implement projects effectively.

## Goal 1.3: Improved GCF MRV capacities & monitoring of climate vulnerabilities

Goal 1.3 focuses on improving the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Monitoring, Reporting, and Verification (MRV) capacities and enhancing the monitoring of climate vulnerabilities. The objective is to ensure that the GCF-funded projects are monitored effectively through various initiatives such as the National Adaptation Plan (NAP), the National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA), and other available resources.

### Activity 1.3.1: Develop capacity in the country on MRV systems

Activity 1.3.1 aims to develop capacity in the country on Monitoring, Reporting, and Verification (MRV) systems. The objective is to enhance the capacity of national and local governments to monitor and report on climate change mitigation and adaptation efforts effectively.

### Sub-activity 1.3.1.1: Capacity assessment and development in capturing, recording, tracking, and reporting

Sub-activity 1.3.1.1 focuses on capacity assessment and development in capturing, recording, tracking, and reporting climate change mitigation and adaptation efforts. The objective is to enhance the capacity of national and local governments to monitor and report on these efforts effectively.

The plan of daily on-farm visits will also help to identify any problems or challenges that the farmers may face in using the MIV tools.

### Sub-activity 1.3.1: Develop MIV tools and design a policy dialogue to align with national MIV for the fresh milk value chain

The development of MIV tools and the design of a policy dialogue will be aligned with national MIV policies and strategies. The development of MIV tools will also take into account the needs and preferences of the farmers in the four countries. The design of a policy dialogue will also involve stakeholders at the national level, including government agencies, private sector organizations, and civil society organizations.

### Sub-activity 1.3.2: Improve GNI estimation methodologies to daily value chain data

On-farm visits will be conducted to collect data on the number of animals, their weights, and milk yields. This data will be used to improve the estimation methodologies for Gross National Income (GNI). The improved methodologies will also take into account the seasonality of milk production and other factors that may affect GNI estimates.

### Sub-activity 1.3.3: Collect quality data on terms to enhance Tier 2 daily value chain data

The collection of quality data on terms will be enhanced by conducting surveys and interviews with farmers, processors, and consumers in the four countries. The collected data will also be used to improve the accuracy and reliability of Tier 2 daily value chain data.

### Sub-activity 1.3.4: Conduct surveys and on-farm visits to generate Tier 3 emission factors for baseline and improved dairy under various feed, animal health, and manure management scenarios

Surveys and on-farm visits will be conducted to collect data on the various scenarios of feed, animal health, and manure management. The collected data will also be used to develop emission factors for different scenarios.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal v.1.1

## Sub-activity 1.3.1: Training on the Global Livestock Environmental Assessment Model (GLEAM) and IPCC assessment tool to calculate GHG emissions footprint, and to obtain the national baseline for livestock sector

### Objective
To train stakeholders in the use of GLEAM and IPCC tools within the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Readiness Preparation Proposal (RPP) system. This will enable countries to calculate their livestock sector's greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions footprint, which is a requirement for accessing GCF funding.

## Sub-activity 1.3.2: Development of National Livestock Emission Factors

### Objective
To develop national emission factors that are representative of the country’s livestock sector and to provide the necessary data to improve the accuracy of GHG emissions calculations. This will involve collecting and analyzing data on livestock populations, feed types, manure management practices, and other relevant factors.

## Sub-activity 1.3.3: Development of National Livestock Emission Factors

### Objective
To develop national emission factors that are representative of the country’s livestock sector and to provide the necessary data to improve the accuracy of GHG emissions calculations. This will involve collecting and analyzing data on livestock populations, feed types, manure management practices, and other relevant factors.

## Activity 1.3.4: Facilitate knowledge certification and reduce barriers to access carbon finance

### Objective
To facilitate knowledge certification and reduce barriers to accessing carbon finance for the livestock sector. This will involve developing training materials and resources that are tailored to the specific needs of the livestock sector, and providing technical assistance to support the implementation of these materials.

## Output 1.3.4: Knowledge products developed and disseminated

### Objective
To develop knowledge products that are tailored to the specific needs of the livestock sector and to disseminate these products through targeted outreach activities. This will involve developing training manuals, guidelines, and other resources that are designed to support the implementation of GLEAM and IPCC tools within the GCF RPP system.

#### Sub-activity 1.4.3.1: Promotion of climate-smart dairy farming practices and technologies to increase productivity, efficiency, and sustainability of dairy production systems.

This sub-activity will promote the adoption of climate-smart dairy farming practices and technologies that are appropriate for the local context and can be easily adopted by smallholder farmers. The objective is to improve the livelihoods of smallholder dairy farmers and contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions from the dairy sector.

#### Sub-activity 1.4.3.2: Development and implementation of a climate-smart dairy value chain strategy.

This sub-activity will develop and implement a climate-smart dairy value chain strategy that includes the promotion of climate-smart practices, technologies, and policies to support the development of a sustainable dairy sector. The objective is to improve the productivity, efficiency, and sustainability of the dairy value chain and contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions.

### Sub-activity 1.4.3.3: Capacity building for dairy farmers and other stakeholders in climate-smart practices and technologies.

This sub-activity will provide training and capacity building for dairy farmers and other stakeholders on climate-smart practices and technologies. The objective is to improve the knowledge and skills of dairy farmers and other stakeholders, enabling them to adopt and implement climate-smart practices and technologies that can improve productivity, efficiency, and sustainability of the dairy sector.

### Sub-activity 1.4.3.4: Promotion of sustainable feed management practices for dairy cattle.

Commissioner's note regarding the quality and the economy, capacity of the request (former) donor Component 3.

### 120

Activities under this Component are intensive, with scale-up and replication potential. Strengthening the very poor countries' capacities to implement adaptation technologies and practices, and promote their sustainable use, is a key objective. This will facilitate the development of local expertise and capacity in adaptation planning and implementation. It will also enhance the feasibility of future adaptation projects. The Component will support the development of national strategies for adaptation, including vulnerability assessments, and the preparation of National Adaptation Programmes of Action (NAPAs). It will also support the development of regional cooperation on adaptation.

### 125

Activities under this Component are feasible. The strengthening of local capacities will significantly enhance the ability to implement adaptation measures at national level. This will be achieved through technical assistance, capacity building and training, as well as through the provision of financial resources for small-scale projects. The Component will also support regional cooperation on adaptation, including the development of regional strategies and action plans.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## Sub-activity 2.1.3.0 Enhance the capacity and capability of daily operations, M&COs and MCPs

The expansion of regular undertaking of activities and higher frequency of data collection will lead to improved quality of data collected and used for decision making. This will enhance the ability of the Programme to support investments in M&COs and MCPs as well as unplanned/emergent investments. The Programme will also be able to provide technical assistance to countries to improve their capacity to monitor, report and verify (MRV) systems.

## Sub-activity 2.1.4.0 Greenhouse gas emissions accounting

Greenhouse gas emissions accounting is a key component of the Programme’s operations. The Programme will support the development of national greenhouse gas inventory systems and provide technical assistance for the implementation of the Paris Agreement. This includes support to countries in developing their nationally determined contributions (NDCs) and long-term strategies.

## Activity 2.1.3 Build and enhance productive capacities using digital value chains

The Programme will promote green production activities and build local capabilities at national, regional and global levels. It will also support the development of green supply chains and improve access to markets for small-scale producers. The Programme will provide technical assistance to countries in developing their own green industrial policies.

## Sub-activity 2.1.3.1 Strengthen comprehensive capacity building

The Programme will build on existing capacities at national, regional and global levels. It will support the development of new capacities and the provision of training and technical assistance. The Programme will also provide opportunities for countries to learn from each other and to share best practices.

## Sub-activity 2.1.3.2 Promote digitalisation and traceability of daily operations

The Programme will promote the use of digital technologies in daily operations, including with respect to finance, procurement and reporting. It will support the development of digital platforms for tracking and reporting on climate change mitigation and adaptation activities.

## Sub-activity 4.1.3.0 Build high-level political support after the COP

The Programme will build on existing capacities at national, regional and global levels. It will support the development of new capacities and the provision of training and technical assistance. The Programme will also provide opportunities for countries to learn from each other and to share best practices.

## B

Eight new climate change departments and agencies focus on the four core functions: The Programme will enhance the development and implementation of integrated planning and monitoring systems (IMS) to be used by all departments and agencies. This will support the development of a national adaptation plan, a national greenhouse gas emissions inventory, and a national climate change policy. These documents will be developed through a series of workshops and consultations with key stakeholders including government ministries, non-governmental organizations, and community groups.

### Activity 2.1.3: Promote renewable energy (RE) and waste management technologies

The Programme will promote the use of renewable energy sources such as solar, wind, hydro, geothermal, and biomass to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. This will include the development of policies and regulations for clean energy and waste management, as well as the provision of technical assistance and capacity building for local communities.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.3.1: Assess the need and viability of renewable energy and waste management technologies

The Programme will conduct a comprehensive assessment of the feasibility and potential impact of various renewable energy and waste management technologies in each county. This will include an analysis of the technical, economic, social, and environmental factors that affect the adoption of these technologies.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.3.2: Support the development of clean energy and waste management policies

The Programme will support the development of national policies and regulations for renewable energy and waste management. This will include the provision of technical assistance to government agencies, as well as the promotion of public awareness campaigns to encourage the adoption of these technologies.

#### Sub-activity 2.1.3.3: Organize capacity building in energy efficiency and waste management

The Programme will organize training workshops and seminars for local communities on renewable energy and waste management technologies. This will include the provision of technical assistance and capacity building for local communities to implement these technologies.

### Output 2.2: Breathing, water health, land, and food: finance management design

#### Activity 2.2.1: Promote the adoption of climate smart dairy breeding enhanced climate change breeding and animal improvement with an improved technology which enhances the resilience of the cow/water/pregnancy environment

The Programme will promote the adoption of climate-smart dairy farming practices that enhance the resilience of cows to climate change. This will include the provision of technical assistance and capacity building for local communities, as well as the promotion of public awareness campaigns to encourage the adoption of these practices.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1: PAGE 4

## B

From Jerry, are many variables to high temperatures and discharges to complex changes, but present a significant opportunity to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. The proposal is to develop a comprehensive approach to climate change mitigation and adaptation that includes the following components:

- **Sub-activity 2.2.1.1** High good quality dairy cattle genetics, suitable to extreme climatic conditions and diseases.
- **Sub-activity 2.2.1.2** Increase in breeding and artificial insemination (AI) centres.
- **Sub-activity 2.2.1.3** Breed improvement and selection of local breeds.
- **Sub-activity 2.2.1.4** Use of improved forages and fodder crops.
- **Sub-activity 2.2.1.5** Improve the management of dairy cattle.

### Sub-activity 2.2.1.1 High good quality dairy cattle genetics, suitable to extreme climatic conditions and diseases

This sub-activity will focus on improving the genetic potential of local breeds through crossbreeding with exotic breeds that are more tolerant to high temperatures and diseases. This will involve the importation of high-quality semen from countries such as France, USA, UK, and Australia. The semen will be distributed to AI centres in Kenya for use by farmers.

### Sub-activity 2.2.1.2 Increase in breeding and artificial insemination (AI) centres

This sub-activity aims to increase the number of AI centres in Kenya from the current 50 to 100 within the next five years. This will involve the establishment of new AI centres in areas where there are no existing ones, as well as the upgrading of existing centres.

### Sub-activity 2.2.1.3 Breed improvement and selection of local breeds

This sub-activity aims to improve the genetic potential of local breeds through selective breeding and the use of AI. This will involve the identification of superior animals within each breed and their use as sires in AI programmes.

### Sub-activity 2.2.1.4 Use of improved forages and fodder crops

This sub-activity aims to improve the productivity of dairy cattle by increasing the availability of high-quality forage and fodder crops. This will involve the promotion of improved varieties of forage and fodder crops, as well as the provision of technical assistance to farmers in their production.

### Sub-activity 2.2.1.5 Improve the management of dairy cattle

This sub-activity aims to improve the productivity of dairy cattle by improving their management. This will involve the promotion of best practices in dairy cattle management, as well as the provision of technical assistance to farmers.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1: PAGE 4

## Sub-activity 2.2.1.1 Make certified biomass fuel available to private and community multipliers

### Objective
Provide small-scale biomass fuel production units with certified biomass fuel to support the development of local markets for biomass fuel. This will enable the widespread adoption of biomass fuel by households, small enterprises, and schools.

### Activities
- Establish partnerships with local biomass fuel producers.
- Facilitate access to certified biomass fuel for private and community multipliers.
- Develop and implement a marketing strategy to promote the use of certified biomass fuel.

## Sub-activity 2.2.1.2 Support multiplication of climate-resilient and the fuel-efficient stoves and ovens

### Objective
Promote the adoption of climate-resilient and fuel-efficient stoves and ovens among households, small enterprises, and schools to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from biomass fuel use.

### Activities
- Conduct awareness campaigns on the benefits of using climate-resilient and fuel-efficient stoves and ovens.
- Provide training and technical assistance to local artisans for the production of climate-resilient and fuel-efficient stoves and ovens.
- Facilitate access to financing for households, small enterprises, and schools to purchase climate-resilient and fuel-efficient stoves and ovens.

## Sub-activity 2.2.1.3 Build capacity of daily farmers on climate-smart agriculture practices

### Objective
Enhance the capacity of daily farmers on climate-smart agriculture practices that improve soil health, increase crop yields, and enhance resilience to climate change.

### Activities
- Conduct training sessions on climate-smart agriculture practices.
- Provide access to climate-smart seeds and planting materials.
- Facilitate access to financing for daily farmers to adopt climate-smart agriculture practices.

## Sub-activity 2.2.1.4 Address water for animals and fuel production in intensive and semi-intensive systems

### Objective
Improve the management of water resources for animal husbandry and fuel production in intensive and semi-intensive systems.

### Activities
- Conduct assessments of water resources in intensive and semi-intensive systems.
- Develop and implement water management plans to improve water use efficiency.
- Provide training on efficient water use practices for animal husbandry and fuel production.

## Activity 2.2.2 Provide climate-smart funding practices to enable, empower, and reduce emissions

### Objective
Promote climate-smart funding practices that enable, empower, and reduce greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture, forestry, and other land uses.

### Activities
- Develop and implement climate-smart funding policies.
- Facilitate access to financing for climate-smart projects in agriculture, forestry, and other land uses.
- Conduct monitoring and evaluation of the impact of climate-smart funding practices on greenhouse gas emissions.

## Sub-activity 2.2.1.5 Review existing state-specific adaptation plans and test their alignment with NDCs

### Objective
Review and update existing state-specific adaptation plans to ensure they align with national climate change policies and commitments under the Paris Agreement.

### Activities
- Conduct a review of existing state-specific adaptation plans.
- Facilitate stakeholder consultations to update adaptation plans.
- Develop and implement monitoring and evaluation frameworks for updated adaptation plans.

## Sub-activity 2.2.1.6 Review and update existing state-specific mitigation plans

### Objective

## Sub-activity 2.3.1.2 Build capacity on appropriate feed formulation in each country

The Programme will provide training and technical assistance to build capacity for appropriate feed formulation in each country. This will include:

- Training on feed formulation techniques
- Provision of manuals and guidelines
- On-the-job training with local experts

## Sub-activity 2.3.1.3 Domestic commercial feed formulation that reduces antennae methane emissions in the feed sector

The Programme will work with the feed industry to develop and promote the use of domestic commercial feed formulations that reduce methane emissions from livestock. This will include:

- Research and development of new feed formulations
- Promotion of existing low-methane feed formulations
- Collaboration with local feed manufacturers to produce and market low-methane feeds

## Activity 2.3.1 Enhance sustainable poultry and dairy yield in local communities

Local poultry and dairy farming is a source of income (GNI) and rural livelihood (NCD) enhancement, as well as food security and nutrition improvement. The Programme will support the development of sustainable poultry and dairy farming practices that enhance productivity and profitability. This will include:

- Training on modern poultry and dairy farming techniques
- Provision of improved breeds and feeds
- Promotion of best management practices

## Sub-activity 2.3.1.4 Improve management of intensive and semi-intensive dairy production systems

The Programme will provide training and technical assistance to improve the management of intensive and semi-intensive dairy production systems. This will include:

- Training on modern dairy farming techniques
- Provision of improved breeds and feeds
- Promotion of best management practices

## Sub-activity 2.3.1.5 Promote enhanced animal housing

The Programme will promote enhanced animal housing with the potential of reducing soil and air pollution. Local expertise and appropriate housing designs will be promoted to enhance animal welfare, reduce environmental impact, and improve productivity.

## Sub-activity 2.3.1.6 Nature-based solutions for extensive systems (pastoral and semi-pastoral farming systems)

The Programme will promote nature-based solutions for extensive farming systems, including:

- Promotion of sustainable grazing practices
- Improvement of pasture quality and quantity
- Promotion of integrated crop-livestock systems

## Sub-activity 2.3.1.7 Promote improved breeding programs

The Programme will support the development and implementation of improved breeding programs to enhance productivity and profitability in the livestock sector.

## Sub-activity 2.3.1.8 Promote enhanced animal health management

The Programme will promote enhanced animal health management practices, including:

- Promotion of preventive healthcare
- Training on modern veterinary practices
- Provision of veterinary services

## Sub-activity 2.3.1.9 Promote sustainable use of natural resources

The Programme will support the sustainable use of natural resources in livestock production, including:

- Promotion of efficient water use
- Improvement of soil fertility
- Promotion of sustainable land use practices

## Sub-activity 2.3.1.10 Promote enhanced marketing and value addition

The Programme will promote enhanced marketing and value addition for livestock products, including:

- Development of local markets
- Promotion of value-added products
- Support to small and medium enterprises (SMEs) in the livestock sector

## Sub-activity 2.3.1.11 Promote gender equality and social inclusion

- Training on gender-responsive livestock practices

## B

### Activity 2.2.6: Build capacity for ex-ante use of bioeconomic and biopagas

Boosts domestic ultra modernization efforts done through bio-economic management, which is expected to be implemented in the organic waste during post-harvest handling. This will help to reduce the amount of waste generated by the community and improve the quality of life. The program will also provide training on how to use bioeconomic tools for waste management. The program will be implemented by the Department of Energy and Environment (DEE) with support from the Ministry of Energy and Environment (MEE). The program will be implemented in collaboration with local governments, non-governmental organizations, and other stakeholders.

### Sub-activity 2.2.6.1: Support capacity building on bioeconomic management

The program will build the skills and knowledge needed to implement bioeconomic tools for waste management. This includes training on how to use bioeconomic tools for waste management, including self-working technologies.

### Sub-activity 2.2.6.3: Support incentives to beneficiaries of the bioeconomic level

The program will provide financial support to beneficiaries who are implementing bioeconomic tools for waste management.

### Output 2.2: Faeces and grasslands management improved

May also provide capacity building on how to use bioeconomic tools for waste management, which can be implemented in collaboration with local governments. This includes training on how to use bioeconomic tools for waste management, including self-working technologies. The program will also provide financial support to beneficiaries who are implementing bioeconomic tools for waste management.

### Activity 2.3.1: Strengthen governance structures for community grazing sites

The program will strengthen the capacity of local governments to manage community grazing sites. This includes training on how to use bioeconomic tools for waste management, including self-working technologies. The program will also provide financial support to beneficiaries who are implementing bioeconomic tools for waste management.

### Sub-activity 2.3.1.1: Participatory management planning in selected districts

The program will provide capacity building on how to use bioeconomic tools for waste management, including self-working technologies. This includes training on how to use bioeconomic tools for waste management, including self-working technologies. The program will also provide financial support to beneficiaries who are implementing bioeconomic tools for waste management.

* Implementing Agency: DEE
* Duration: 2019-2023
* The Long-Term Plan, SDG 15, and the National Strategy for Sustainable Development (NSDS) 2016-2030.
* The program will be implemented in collaboration with local governments, non-governmental organizations, and other stakeholders.

### Additional Information

* The program will provide capacity building on how to use bioeconomic tools for waste management, including self-working technologies. This includes training on how to use bioeconomic tools for waste management, including self-working technologies.
* The program will also provide financial support to beneficiaries who are implementing bioeconomic tools for waste management.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.1

## Sub-activity 2.3.2.1 Restorative of degraded landscapes, existing forests to protect

The overall outcome is to restore and maintain degraded landscapes, existing forests, and other ecosystems. The specific outcomes are:

- Restore degraded landscapes.
- Protect existing forests.

### Outputs
- Restored landscapes.
- Protected forests.

## Sub-activity 2.3.2.1 Training on restorative and pasture management

The Program will develop a training program for the restoration of degraded lands, including pasture management and soil conservation techniques. The training will be conducted by local experts in collaboration with international organizations. The training will include:

- Techniques for restoring degraded landscapes.
- Soil conservation methods.
- Pasture management practices.

## Sub-activity 2.3.2.2 Restore pastures and pasture revitalization

This sub-activity focuses on grazing land restoration, including pasture revitalization techniques such as:

- Soil improvement.
- Planting native grasses.
- Fencing to protect new growth.

## Sub-activity 2.3.2.3 Water availability - improving the quality, storage areas were for water harvesting and soil moisture conservation

This sub-activity focuses on improving water availability by implementing various water harvesting techniques such as:

- Building dams and reservoirs.
- Improving irrigation systems.
- Implementing soil moisture conservation practices.

## COMPONENT B: GREEN CLIMATE FINANCE FACILITY (GCFF)

### Output 3.1

To support the achievement of the program objectives and impact, GCFF will assist in the following:

- Provide technical assistance to the Program for the implementation of the project.
- Facilitate access to finance from international financial institutions.

### GCFF Financial Resources

GCFF will provide financial resources to support the implementation of the Program. The specific outcomes are:

- Improved water availability.
- Restored landscapes and protected forests.

### Flexibility Clause

Granted for inputs or the specific outputs to be provided, in case of unforeseen circumstances that affect the achievement of the overall objective. In such cases, the resources will be reallocated within an equivalent budget to ensure the overall objective is achieved.

## Submitting grant proposals to related donor companies to support the GOFF funding and key sector investment

### 154
The GOFF could benefit from DMAI because it will be sent by FAO to the tax departments in USD and by the governments through EEUs to local implementation partners in local currencies, and all the funding operations would be managed by the GOFF. This is a very important step for the GOFF to be able to manage its own funds and to have a direct relationship with DMAI.

### 155
To achieve this Management objective, Component 3 activities are structured into two outputs: (Output 3.1: Effective Grant Financing for Daily Value Chains)

## Activity 3.1.1: GOFF Operationalization

In each of the four participating countries, a GOFF will be created to manage the funds and ensure that they are used effectively. This will include setting up a legal entity, establishing bank accounts, and developing internal controls and procedures for managing the funds. The GOFF will also be responsible for ensuring that the funds are used in accordance with the terms of the grant agreement. In addition, the GOFF will be responsible for providing technical assistance to the grantees and for monitoring and evaluating the impact of the grants.

### 156
The Wholesale Benefits of people who have no access to the formal financial system, which will be addressed by the GOFF funding, will be provided with a range of services that are not currently available to them. This includes access to credit, savings accounts, and insurance products. The GOFF will also provide technical assistance to help these people build their capacity and improve their livelihoods.

### 157
Direct Benefits for smallholder farmers will be achieved through the Wholesale Benefits and Direct FAO Key outcomes in the following ways: (i) Smallholder farmers will have access to credit, savings accounts, and insurance products that are not currently available to them. This will help them to invest in their farms, improve their productivity, and increase their incomes. (ii) The GOFF will also provide technical assistance to help smallholder farmers build their capacity and improve their livelihoods.

### 158
Targeted Borrowers and Key Leading Products. As outlined, the GOFF funding operations under this component will target specific groups of borrowers who are engaged in key leading products, and make them more competitive. This is a very important outcome because it will help to increase the competitiveness of these products and improve their access to markets.

The GCF result area targets will be tracked to ensure that results for wide adherence to transparent and accountable governance structures are achieved. The GCF will also track the use of funds and provide feedback on the progress made towards achieving the targets.

## 159. Landing Terms to Lead Partners
The Dual-GCF Financing package is designed to encourage the Private Sector to invest in climate change mitigation and adaptation projects. The terms have been developed by the GCF, the World Bank Group (WBG), and the Government of France (GOF). The WBG will provide co-financing from its own resources or from resources mobilized for highly relevant programs that benefit from business continuity. The GOF will provide a grant to cover the costs of the technical assistance required by the project.

## 160. Two GCF Financing Plans
The Dual-GCF Financing package is illustrated in Figure 17b. Concessional finance is required for projects with high climate and development impacts, such as renewable energy, energy efficiency, and sustainable transport. The concessional finance will be provided by the GCF through a combination of grants and low-interest loans. The private sector will provide co-financing to ensure that the project is financially viable.

To satisfy the aforementioned market-based interest in a GCF-funded “SAFE” project, we collaborate with the World Bank and the International Finance Corporation (IFC) to develop a SAFE project concept note. The project will be implemented by PAD Smallholder Dairy Performance Contracting (PAD-SDPC), which includes the models of the mainstreamed GCF-funded project. The project will be designed to ensure that the benefits from the SAFE project are shared with the local communities and that the project is aligned with the objectives of the Green Climate Fund (GCF).
